    Comparing the matrix elements (261) with those of the one-particle operators of the
Virasoro generators and the KS approximants (223), we see that the convergence results of
Section 4.2.4 apply to the approximation of the U (1)-current by (257) in the scaling limit.
In fact the situation is considerably better than for the Virasoro generators, because jk is a
unitary momentum translation and no discretisation of a derivative is involved. Two points
nevertheless require care, and we treat them first.
                                                                               (N )
    The modification of Section 4.2.2 is needed here as well. As it stands, jk of (260) is
the cyclic shift on ΓN,− : for the |k| Lπ momenta n ∈ ΓN,− with n + k ∈   / ΓN,− it produces
n + k − ε2πN
             instead of  n + k.  For  k > 0 these are the momenta   at the upper edge of the
Brillouin zone, and the wrap-around sends them to the lower edge — that is, across the
                                     (N )
Fermi point n = 0. Consequently j̃k acquires off-diagonal matrix elements of modulus one
relative to the Hardy decomposition, and
                                 ⃦ (N )                      )︁ 1

which does not tend to 0. The third hypothesis of Lemma 4.19, and with it the estimate
(224) in the form used in Theorem 4.21, is therefore unavailable for the unmodified currents.
                          (N )

exactly as in (162); j k         is a partial isometry, it still satisfies j k              = j −k and the imple-
                                                                   (N )

so that none of the asymptotic statements is affected.
    What is gained. With the modification, the one-particle analysis becomes exact rather
than asymptotic.

Remark 5.3 (Currents versus Virasoro generators). It is worth collecting how much simpler
the current is. (a) No regularity of s is needed for (270), in contrast with Lemma 4.6
and Theorem 4.21. (b) The convergence holds on D(N) rather than on a core of finite-
particle vectors, and the relevant bound involves the number operator instead of the conformal
Hamiltonian — no analogue of Lemma 4.14 is required, jk being bounded. (c) The Schwinger
cocycle contributes no error at any finite scale, by Lemma 5.1 (ii); the central charge c = 1 of
                                                  72
(256) is reproduced exactly for επN > 2|k|, not merely in the limit. (d) The rate is εδN with no
cap, the only error being the Brillouin-zone truncation. Numerically (with L = π and k = 3)
the measured rates of ∥˜j k − jk ∥hδ →h0 are 0.00, 0.51, 1.02, 2.03, 3.05 at δ = 0, 12 , 1, 2, 3, the
                             (N )

value at δ = 0 being exactly 1; and the off-diagonal Hilbert-Schmidt norms are exactly 0,
                  1
against (|k| Lπ ) 2 for the unmodified currents, cf. (267).

                                                                        73
6      Approximation of correlation functions
The convergence results of the previous sections for the approximation of Virasoro generator,
their unitaries and the associated Bogoliubov transformations allow for the approximation
of (chiral) correlation functions of the resulting fermionic continuum field theories in the
scaling limit. Explicit error bounds can be obtained along the lines outlined in the last
paragraph of Section 4.2.4.

Fermion correlation function. Using either the wavelet or the momentum-cutoff renor-
malization group, we obtain the following approximation theorem concerning the dynamical
                                                                                       (±)
(chiral) correlation functions of fermions. We state the theorem for complex fermions A∞,± ,
                                                                                                            (±)
but an analogous statement holds for the Majorana fermions B∞,± as is clear from the
structure of the proofs in Section 4.2.4.

    We can now make Theorem 6.1 quantitative for the case relevant to a simulation, namely
the chiral time evolution. Two features of the momentum-cutoff renormalization group make
                                                                           (N )
this possible without any intermediate scale: the one-particle generator ℓ±,0 is a Fourier
multiplier, so the lattice dynamics does not move momenta at all, and αN  M maps into the

span of {el }l∈ΓM,± . The renormalized observable therefore stays supported on momenta
|l| ≤ επM for all times, and only the deviation of the vacuum symbols on those momenta
enters.

span{el }l∈ΓM,± and AdUN (t) (a(ξ)) = a(eitℓ±,0 ξ) with ℓ±,0 a Fourier multiplier, ON is a
monomial of degree dA + dB in generators a(ξ) with ∥ξ∥ ≤ 1 and ξˆ supported in ΓM,± , for
every t. The determinant estimate (136) of Lemma 3.17 therefore applies with the symbol
deviation taken over ΓM,± only, and gives the first term of (278). The two cases in (279)
are (141) and, for the chiral subalgebras, the corresponding restriction: by (65) and (128),

Remark 6.4 (Quantum simulation: the error budget). Collecting the pieces, we can now
state what a simulation of a chiral free-fermion CFT costs. Let η > 0 be the target accuracy
for the dynamical correlation functions of Proposition 6.3 on a time interval |t| ≤ T , and
write d := dA + dB .
    (1) The two error sources scale in N − M , not in N . By (278) and εN = 2−N L,
both terms are governed by the ratio εN /εM = 2−(N −M ) — the dynamics term because
           −3
ΘM ε2N ≍ εM2 2−2(N −M ) . This is as it should be: M fixes the physical resolution at which the
observables are prepared and read out, and N − M is the number of additional refinement
steps.
    (2) Chiral observables are quadratically cheaper. On the full two-component algebra
AM,± the ground-state term of (279) is of first order, and it then dominates the second-order
dynamics term. The obstruction is precisely the chiral entanglement (65) of the massless
                                              (+)
lattice vacuum: the off-diagonal block p± PλN =0 p∓ is of order εN k, whereas its continuum
                                                              (±)
counterpart vanishes. Restricted to a chiral subalgebra AM,± — which is where the conformal
structure lives, cf. Section 4.2.3 — the deviation is sin( 41 εN l)2 and hence of second order,
matching the dynamics term.
    (3) Resource count. The simulation is carried out on the transverse XY chain (81) on
ΛN +1 , so the number of qubits is #ΛN +1 = 2LN +1 ∝ 2N . For chiral observables, (278)
gives total error ≲ d (1 + ΘM ε2M T ) 2−2(N −M ) , so that η-accuracy requires
                                                                     (︁ √︁
   N − M ≥ 12 log2 C d (1 + T )/η ,                    #qubits = O 2M d (1 + T )/η . (280)
                     (︁            )︁                                                 )︁
                                           hence

                                                    77
The prefactor 2M is fixed by the two constraints of Remark 4.27: M < N (Nyquist-Shannon)
and εM (2K − 1) ≲ L, i.e. 2M ≳ 2K − 1, so that s(εM ) is well localized in SL1 . Since the rate
ε2N used above requires 2-regularity, Remark 3.12 gives K ≥ 5 and hence M ≥ 4; the value
δ = 2 in (250) itself is unconditional, no scaling function entering the momentum-cutoff
estimates.
    (4) What one loses along the wavelet route. With the wavelet renormalization group the
generator error is O(εN ) rather than O(ε2N ), by Remark 4.7, so (280) degrades to #qubits =
O(2M d(1 + T )/η) — a quadratic loss. The re-centred comparison of Remark 3.5 restores
the exponent 21 . Localization in real space, which is what the wavelet group buys, is therefore
paid for in qubits unless one re-centres.
    (5) Currents are cheaper still. For the U (1)- and WZW-currents, Lemma 5.1 gives
a generator error O(εδN ) with δ arbitrary, subject only to (δ + 12 )-regularity of s, and no
Schwinger-cocycle error at any finite scale.
                                        √︁    The ground-state term of (279) then becomes the
sole bottleneck, and #qubits = O(2M d(1 + T )/η) is attained already for chiral observables
of the current algebra without any condition beyond 2-regularity.

Virasoro correlation functions Since the (smeared) KS approximants converge to the
(smeared) Virasoro generators in the scaling limit representations π± using the momentum-
cutoff renormalization group (possibly in combination with the wavelet renormalization
group for the smearing functions) by Theorems 4.13 & 4.21, we obtain an analogue of
Theorem 6.1 for correlation functions involving the Virasoro generators or their associated
unitaries, cp. [97]. A subtle difference arises from the fact that the KS approximants are
only strongly operator-convergent on the Fock-space vectors with finite particle number in
momentum space, Falg  a (Dstd ), and not operator-convergent in the sense of Corollary 6.2.
                                                      (±)
Again, we state the theorem for complex fermions A∞,± , but an analogous statement holds
                                       (±)
for the Majorana fermions B∞,± .

Remark 6.7 (Mixed correlation functions). Theorem 6.1 & 6.5 can be combined to obtain
convergent expressions for mixed correlation functions, i.e. those containing insertions of
               (±)              (±)
operators in A∞,± respectively B∞,± , Virasoro generators, WZW currents and their asso-
ciated unitaries.


Acknowledgements
The authors would like to thank R. F. Werner for valuable discussion about inductive
limits in quantum theory. AS would like to thank Y. Tanimoto for helpful discussions
concerning the essential self-adjointness of smeared Virasoro generators. Moreover, the
authors would like to extend their gratitude to the unknown reviewers providing valuable
feedback on a previous version of the manuscript, which improved the presentation and
clarified the relation to existing work. Special thanks are extended to one of the reviewers
for suggesting to clarify the relation with the Temperley-Lieb algebra and suggesting the
case of symplectic fermions for future work. This work was supported, in part, by the DFG
through SFB 1227 (DQ-mat), Quantum Valley Lower Saxony, and funded by the Deutsche
Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG, German Research Foundation) under Germanys Excellence
Strategy EXC-2123 QuantumFrontiers 390837967.
